Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm anxious, exactly. I mean, I definitely like to stick to my routine, but that's just because I want to stay disciplined, you know? I hope that's okay to say. 

I guess I do think about my weight a bit more than I used to. It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. Almost like a little panic, I guess? 

It's silly, I know. But I just get so worried that I've completely blown it, and that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I'm out of control. Which I'm not, I promise. I just want to look the part of a leader. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.

I'm still doing my job just fine, I promise. I wouldn't ever let anything put the crew at risk. \n\nI guess... well, it hasn't really caused distress, exactly. But, um, I have started ducking out of station meals a bit. You know how it is, the guys will cook a big dinner or order takeout, and lately I just sort of keep to myself when they do that. It's just easier to stick to my own stuff that way, right? \n\nBut I guess it does mean I'm... well, I'm a little less part of the team than I used to be. Which I know probably doesn't sound great, and I feel a little bad about it. But work-wise, everything is totally fine. Is that... is that the kind of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about food, your weight, or your shape quite a bit, or feeling anxious if you can't stick to your planned meals?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm anxious, exactly. I mean, I definitely like to stick to my routine, but that's just because I want to stay disciplined, you know? I hope that's okay to say. \n\nI guess I do think about my weight a bit more than I used to. It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you plan, like during those station meals, do you ever find yourself exercising a lot more or cutting back even harder on food afterward to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I might try to just be a little more mindful the next day, you know? Like maybe stick to just the protein shake and skip dinner if I had a big lunch with the guys. I have to stay in shape for the job, so I always make sure I get my training in. But I don't go crazy or anything. I just want to make sure I'm doing the right things to stay fit for duty. I'm actually really careful to make sure that doesn't happen. I mean, even if I have a slightly bigger meal with the crew like I was saying, I never just... lose control or anything. I always know exactly what I'm eating. \n\nI think I'd be really ashamed if I just let myself go and ate a huge amount all at once. I hope that's okay to say. \n\nI guess I do think about my weight a bit more than I used to. It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. Almost like a little panic, I guess? \n\nIt's silly, I know. But I just get so worried that I've completely blown it, and that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I'm out of control. Which I'm not, I promise. I just want to look the part of a leader. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you plan, like during those station meals, do you ever find yourself exercising a lot more or cutting back even harder on food afterward to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I might try to just be a little more mindful the next day, you know? Like maybe stick to just the protein shake and skip dinner if I had a big lunch with the guys. But that's just normal balancing things out, right? Like, anyone would do that if they overate. \n\nAnd as for exercising, I just do my regular workouts. I have to stay in shape for the job, so I always make sure I get my training in. But I don't go crazy or anything. I just want to make sure I'm doing the right things to stay fit for duty. That's a healthy way to handle it, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you had planned, like during those station meals, do you ever make yourself vomit or take laxatives, diuretics, or any other medications to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. \n\nUm... I know some people do, but that just seems really... well, extreme, I guess? I couldn't risk doing that to my body when the crew relies on me. \n\nI just stick to, you know, being careful the next day like I mentioned. I promise I don't do any of that other stuff. Um... that's a good question. I guess... I'm not totally sure?

It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. Almost like a little panic, I guess? \n\nIt's silly, I know. But I just get so worried that I've completely blown it, and that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I'm out of control. Which I'm not, I promise. I just want to look the part of a leader. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you plan, like during those station meals, do you ever find yourself exercising a lot more or cutting back even harder on food afterward to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I might try to just be a little more mindful the next day, you know? Like maybe stick to just the protein shake and skip dinner if I had a big lunch with the guys. But that's just normal balancing things out, right? Like, anyone would do that if they overate. \n\nAnd as for exercising, I just do my regular workouts. I have to stay in shape for the job, so I always make sure I get my training in. But I don't go crazy or anything. I just want to make sure I'm doing the right things to stay fit for duty. That's a healthy way to handle it, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you had planned, like during those station meals, do you ever make yourself vomit or take laxatives, diuretics, or any other medications to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. \n\nUm... I know some people do, but that just seems really... well, extreme, I guess? And I have to be ready for calls at the firehouse, so throwing up or taking pills like that would totally mess up my system. I couldn't risk doing that to my body when the crew relies on me. \n\nI just stick to, you know, being careful the next day like I mentioned. I promise I don't do any of that other stuff. That's a good thing, right? I'm really trying to be smart about how I handle all this, I swear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a much larger amount of food in a short period than you intended, like feeling like you've lost control and eaten a lot all at once?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not. I'm actually really careful to make sure that doesn't happen. I mean, even if I have a slightly bigger meal with the crew like I was saying, I never just... lose control or anything. I always know exactly what I'm eating. \n\nI think I'd be really ashamed if I just let myself go and ate a huge amount all at once. I try to stay really disciplined, so I keep a pretty tight grip on things. That’s a good sign, right? That I'm not losing control like that? I just want to make sure I'm on the right track with all this.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating this way because you're trying to lose weight or change your body shape?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'm just trying to be the best firefighter I can be, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your main concern is about gaining weight, or is it more about maintaining control and discipline with your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Um... I think it's a little bit of both, maybe? But mostly, it's just about the weight. \n\nI mean, the control part is important because it helps me... well, not gain weight. But the real fear is definitely seeing that number go up. I just... I get so anxious about getting heavier. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to be this obsessed with a few pounds, especially for a guy, but I just want to make sure I'm staying fit for the job. That makes sense, right? It's better to be focused on not gaining weight than to just not care at all, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to eat this way even if your weight stayed exactly the same?\n\nPatient: Um... that's a good question. I don't know, it's hard to imagine just letting go of all the rules. They kind of keep me grounded, I guess. \n\nBut honestly, if the weight wasn't an issue, I'd like to think I could just eat normally like the rest of the guys. I really just want to be healthy and do my job well. It's totally normal to want to stick to a routine once you're used to it, right? I'm not trying to be difficult or anything, I just... I just want to make sure I'm on the right track with all this.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating this way because you're trying to lose weight or change your body shape?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just want to make sure I don't get out of shape. I wouldn't say I'm trying to be, like, super skinny or anything, because I need to be strong for the job. But... yeah, I do worry a lot about gaining. \n\nHonestly, if I step on the scale and it's up even just a single pound—even if I know it's just from extra water or right after one of those big meals with the crew—I get this really tight panic in my chest. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If you were told that your appearance was already exactly how a firefighter should look, and that you were medically healthy, would you still feel the need to control your eating the way you do?\n\nPatient: Um... man, I'd really like to say that I'd just drop the routine completely. Um... yeah, I guess I do still worry about it, even when I know I'm doing all the right things with my diet and training. I really try not to let it get to me, because I know I'm putting in the work, but... 

It's like I mentioned before, about checking the mirror before a shift? I just... I get really fixated on my waist and my face. If I don't look lean enough, or sharp enough, it just kind of sits in the back of my mind all day. It really throws off my confidence. I just don't feel as capable or ready to take charge if I feel like I'm looking soft. 

I know it probably sounds silly to get so hung up on something like that when I know I'm fit, but... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself the right way, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound too superficial. Is it normal to get a little stressed about that kind of stuff?

Um... I guess I haven't really timed it or anything. I try to stay focused on my job and everything else I have going on, so it's not like I'm just sitting around obsessing over it all day. I'm usually pretty busy.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the same need to stick to your current eating routine if a doctor told you that your current weight and body composition are perfectly healthy and appropriate for your job?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, I mean, if a doctor told me I was perfectly healthy, I'd really want to believe them. Um... I wouldn't say it's caused any real problems. I'm definitely still doing my job, and I never let it get in the way of a call. I always make sure I'm 100% there for the public and my crew when it counts.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, I hope I'm in the right place for this. \n\nBasically, I guess I'm here because... well, I've been trying to get on the lieutenant track at the firehouse, and I started out just trying to eat a bit cleaner to prepare for that. Which is a good thing, right? Just trying to be healthier. But, um, I guess it sort of turned into me cutting my meals way down. \n\nLike, lately I'll usually just do black coffee and maybe a protein shake during the day, and then a pretty small dinner. And I've been doing that even on shift days, when I know for a fact I've burned way more energy than that. It's probably not a huge deal, but I just want to make sure I'm not messing anything up, I guess? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this way of eating been causing you any distress or affecting your performance at the firehouse or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, my performance is still really good. I'm still doing my job just fine, I promise. I wouldn't ever let anything put the crew at risk. \n\nI guess... well, it hasn't really caused distress, exactly. But, um, I have started ducking out of station meals a bit. You know how it is, the guys will cook a big dinner or order takeout, and lately I just sort of keep to myself when they do that. It's just easier to stick to my own stuff that way, right? \n\nBut I guess it does mean I'm... well, I'm a little less part of the team than I used to be. Which I know probably doesn't sound great, and I feel a little bad about it. But work-wise, everything is totally fine. Is that... is that the kind of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about food, your weight, or your shape quite a bit, or feeling anxious if you can't stick to your planned meals?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm anxious, exactly. I mean, I definitely like to stick to my routine, but that's just because I want to stay disciplined, you know? I hope that's okay to say. \n\nI guess I do think about my weight a bit more than I used to. It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. Almost like a little panic, I guess? \n\nIt's silly, I know. But I just get so worried that I've completely blown it, and that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I'm out of control. Which I'm not, I promise. I just want to look the part of a leader. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you plan, like during those station meals, do you ever find yourself exercising a lot more or cutting back even harder on food afterward to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I might try to just be a little more mindful the next day, you know? Like maybe stick to just the protein shake and skip dinner if I had a big lunch with the guys. But that's just normal balancing things out, right? Like, anyone would do that if they overate. \n\nAnd as for exercising, I just do my regular workouts. I have to stay in shape for the job, so I always make sure I get my training in. But I don't go crazy or anything. I just want to make sure I'm doing the right things to stay fit for duty. That's a healthy way to handle it, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you had planned, like during those station meals, do you ever make yourself vomit or take laxatives, diuretics, or any other medications to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. \n\nUm... I know some people do, but that just seems really... well, extreme, I guess? And I have to be ready for calls at the firehouse, so throwing up or taking pills like that would totally mess up my system. I couldn't risk doing that to my body when the crew relies on me. \n\nI just stick to, you know, being careful the next day like I mentioned. I promise I don't do any of that other stuff. That's a good thing, right? I'm really trying to be smart about how I handle all this, I swear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a much larger amount of food in a short period than you intended, like feeling like you've lost control and eaten a lot all at once?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not. I'm actually really careful to make sure that doesn't happen. I mean, even if I have a slightly bigger meal with the crew like I was saying, I never just... lose control or anything. I always know exactly what I'm eating. \n\nI think I'd be really ashamed if I just let myself go and ate a huge amount all at once. I try to stay really disciplined, so I keep a pretty tight grip on things. That’s a good sign, right? That I'm not losing control like that? I just want to make sure I'm on the right track with all this.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating this way because you're trying to lose weight or change your body shape?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just want to make sure I don't get out of shape. I wouldn't say I'm trying to be, like, super skinny or anything, because I need to be strong for the job. But... yeah, I do worry a lot about gaining. \n\nHonestly, if I step on the scale and it's up even just a single pound—even if I know it's just from extra water or right after one of those big meals with the crew—I get this really tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, you know? I get so worried that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that they'll think I'm just totally out of control. \n\nI just really want to look the part and prove I'm reliable. It's normal to want to stay sharp and not get soft in my line of work, right? I hope that doesn't sound too crazy. I'm just trying to be the best firefighter I can be, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your main concern is about gaining weight, or is it more about maintaining control and discipline with your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Um... I think it's a little bit of both, maybe? But mostly, it's just about the weight. \n\nI mean, the control part is important because it helps me... well, not gain weight. But the real fear is definitely seeing that number go up. I just... I get so anxious about getting heavier. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to be this obsessed with a few pounds, especially for a guy, but I just want to make sure I'm staying fit for the job. That makes sense, right? It's better to be focused on not gaining weight than to just not care at all, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to eat this way even if your weight stayed exactly the same?\n\nPatient: Um... that's a good question. I guess... I'm not totally sure? \n\nIf I knew for a fact my weight wouldn't change at all, maybe I wouldn't have to be quite so strict. But... I don't know, it's hard to imagine just letting go of all the rules. They kind of keep me grounded, I guess. \n\nBut honestly, if the weight wasn't an issue, I'd like to think I could just eat normally like the rest of the guys. I really just want to be healthy and do my job well. It's totally normal to want to stick to a routine once you're used to it, right? I'm not trying to be difficult or anything, I just... I haven't really thought about it like that before. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the same need to stick to your current eating routine if a doctor told you that your current weight and body composition are perfectly healthy and appropriate for your job?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, I mean, if a doctor told me I was perfectly healthy, I'd really want to believe them. I'd want to just be normal and let it go. But... honestly? I think I'd still struggle to drop the routine. \n\nBecause... well, it's not just about the medical numbers on a chart, I guess. It's... I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this, because I don't want to sound vain, but... it's about what I see, too. Like, when I look in the mirror right before heading to the station, I always kind of zero in on my waist, and my face. I just need to check if they look... sharp enough, I guess? \n\nAnd if I feel like they don't—if I look even a little bit soft—it just completely messes with my head. I lose my edge. I just don't feel confident or, like, command-ready for the shift, you know? \n\nI know it probably sounds really shallow to worry about my face and waist like that, especially for a guy. But you kind of have to look the part to feel the part, right? I'm really just trying to be professional and ready for anything. I hope that's okay to say. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If you were told that your appearance was already exactly how a firefighter should look, and that you were medically healthy, would you still feel the need to control your eating the way you do?\n\nPatient: Um... man, I'd really like to say that I'd just drop the routine completely. Like, if someone told me I looked exactly right and was totally healthy, I'd want to just say, 'Okay, great,' and go back to eating whatever. Because I know that's what I should do, right? I really do want to be reasonable about all this. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know for sure. I haven't really been in a position where I felt like I could just... stop worrying about it. So I guess I'm just not sure how I'd actually react in that scenario. \n\nI'd definitely try my absolute best to ease up, though, especially if you or another doctor told me I needed to. I'm really trying to be cooperative and do this right, I hope you know that. Is it okay that I don't have a solid answer for that one?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel significant distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight, even when you know you’re eating healthy and staying fit for the job?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess I do still worry about it, even when I know I'm doing all the right things with my diet and training. I really try not to let it get to me, because I know I'm putting in the work, but... \n\nIt's like I mentioned before, about checking the mirror before a shift? I just... I get really fixated on my waist and my face. If I don't look lean enough, or sharp enough, it just kind of sits in the back of my mind all day. It really throws off my confidence. I just don't feel as capable or ready to take charge if I feel like I'm looking soft. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to get so hung up on something like that when I know I'm fit, but... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself the right way, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound too superficial. Is it normal to get a little stressed about that kind of stuff?\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend thinking about your weight or body shape on a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess I haven't really timed it or anything. I try to stay focused on my job and everything else I have going on, so it's not like I'm just sitting around obsessing over it all day. I'm usually pretty busy.\n\nBut, you know, it does pop into my head... maybe a few times a day? Mostly just around meals, or when I'm getting dressed for a shift and doing that quick mirror check I told you about. I try to just acknowledge it and move on, so it doesn't take up too much of my time. \n\nI really try to keep a good balance and not let it consume my thoughts, because I know that wouldn't be healthy. Does that sound like a normal amount to you? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your weight or body shape even when you're not thinking about food or meals?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess it does happen outside of just eating. Like I was saying about the mirror checks before work... I'll just be getting ready, not thinking about food at all, but I'll catch myself really focusing on my waist or how my face looks. \n\nIf I don't look... I don't know, sharp enough, or lean enough, it just kind of lingers. It really messes with how confident I feel when I walk into the station. I just want to feel command-ready, you know? Like I look the part of someone people can rely on in an emergency. \n\nI try not to let it ruin my whole day, but it definitely distracts me sometimes when I'm just trying to do my job. I hope that doesn't make me sound too vain or anything. It's just about feeling prepared, I think. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your weight or body shape caused any problems in your relationships, at work, or in other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Um... I wouldn't say it's caused any real problems. I'm definitely still doing my job, and I never let it get in the way of a call. I always make sure I'm 100% there for the public and my crew when it counts. \n\nBut... I guess, maybe a little bit at the station? It's just... firefighters eat together a lot, you know? It's a big part of the culture. And lately, I've just been kind of... ducking out of the station meals. When the guys order out or cook a big dinner, I usually just keep to myself and eat my own prepped food in the other room. \n\nI just... I don't want to make a big deal out of it or make them feel weird, so it's easier to just step away. But I guess it does make me feel a little less part of the team than I used to be. I miss hanging out with them in the kitchen. \n\nI'm still a good firefighter, though, I promise. I just prefer to handle my own meals right now. Is that... is that okay? I mean, a lot of guys bring their own food to work, right?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But, um, I guess it sort of turned into me cutting my meals way down. \n\nLike, lately I'll usually just do black coffee and maybe a protein shake during the day, and then a pretty small dinner. And I've been doing that even on shift days, when I know for a fact I've burned way more energy than that. It's probably not a huge deal, but I just want to make sure I'm not messing anything up, I guess? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this way of eating been causing you any distress or affecting your performance at the firehouse or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, my performance is still really good. I'm still doing my job just fine, I promise. I wouldn't ever let anything put the crew at risk. \n\nI guess... well, it hasn't really caused distress, exactly. But, um, I have started ducking out of station meals a bit. You know how it is, the guys will cook a big dinner or order takeout, and lately I just sort of keep to myself when they do that. It's just easier to stick to my own stuff that way, right? \n\nBut I guess it does mean I'm... well, I'm a little less part of the team than I used to be. Which I know probably doesn't sound great, and I feel a little bad about it. But work-wise, everything is totally fine. Is that... is that the kind of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about food, your weight, or your shape quite a bit, or feeling anxious if you can't stick to your planned meals?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm anxious, exactly. I mean, I definitely like to stick to my routine, but that's just because I want to stay disciplined, you know? I hope that's okay to say. \n\nI guess I do think about my weight a bit more than I used to. It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. Almost like a little panic, I guess? \n\nIt's silly, I know. But I just get so worried that I've completely blown it, and that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I'm out of control. Which I'm not, I promise. I just want to look the part of a leader. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you plan, like during those station meals, do you ever find yourself exercising a lot more or cutting back even harder on food afterward to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I might try to just be a little more mindful the next day, you know? Like maybe stick to just the protein shake and skip dinner if I had a big lunch with the guys. But that's just normal balancing things out, right? Like, anyone would do that if they overate. \n\nAnd as for exercising, I just do my regular workouts. I have to stay in shape for the job, so I always make sure I get my training in. But I don't go crazy or anything. I just want to make sure I'm doing the right things to stay fit for duty. That's a healthy way to handle it, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you had planned, like during those station meals, do you ever make yourself vomit or take laxatives, diuretics, or any other medications to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. \n\nUm... I know some people do, but that just seems really... well, extreme, I guess? And I have to be ready for calls at the firehouse, so throwing up or taking pills like that would totally mess up my system. I couldn't risk doing that to my body when the crew relies on me. \n\nI just stick to, you know, being careful the next day like I mentioned. I promise I don't do any of that other stuff. That's a good thing, right? I'm really trying to be smart about how I handle all this, I swear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a much larger amount of food in a short period than you intended, like feeling like you've lost control and eaten a lot all at once?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not. I'm actually really careful to make sure that doesn't happen. I mean, even if I have a slightly bigger meal with the crew like I was saying, I never just... lose control or anything. I always know exactly what I'm eating. \n\nI think I'd be really ashamed if I just let myself go and ate a huge amount all at once. I try to stay really disciplined, so I keep a pretty tight grip on things. That’s a good sign, right? That I'm not losing control like that? I just want to make sure I'm on the right track with all this.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating this way because you're trying to lose weight or change your body shape?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just want to make sure I don't get out of shape. I wouldn't say I'm trying to be, like, super skinny or anything, because I need to be strong for the job. But... yeah, I do worry a lot about gaining. \n\nHonestly, if I step on the scale and it's up even just a single pound—even if I know it's just from extra water or right after one of those big meals with the crew—I get this really tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, you know? I get so worried that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that they'll think I'm just totally out of control. \n\nI just really want to look the part and prove I'm reliable. It's normal to want to stay sharp and not get soft in my line of work, right? I hope that doesn't sound too crazy. I'm just trying to be the best firefighter I can be, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your main concern is about gaining weight, or is it more about maintaining control and discipline with your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Um... I think it's a little bit of both, maybe? But mostly, it's just about the weight. \n\nI mean, the control part is important because it helps me... well, not gain weight. But the real fear is definitely seeing that number go up. I just... I get so anxious about getting heavier. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to be this obsessed with a few pounds, especially for a guy, but I just want to make sure I'm staying fit for the job. That makes sense, right? It's better to be focused on not gaining weight than to just not care at all, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to eat this way even if your weight stayed exactly the same?\n\nPatient: Um... that's a good question. I don't know, it's hard to imagine just letting go of all the rules. They kind of keep me grounded, I guess. \n\nBut honestly, if the weight wasn't an issue, I'd like to think I could just eat normally like the rest of the guys. I really just want to be healthy and do my job well. It's totally normal to want to stick to a routine once you're used to it, right? I'm not trying to be difficult or anything, I just... I haven't really thought about it like that before. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the same need to stick to your current eating routine if a doctor told you that your current weight and body composition are perfectly healthy and appropriate for your job?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, I mean, if a doctor told me I was perfectly healthy, I'd really want to believe them. I'd want to just be normal and let it go. But... honestly? I think I'd still struggle to drop the routine. \n\nBecause... well, it's not just about the medical numbers on a chart, I guess. It's... I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this, because I don't want to sound vain, but... it's about what I see, too. Like, when I look in the mirror right before heading to the station, I always kind of zero in on my waist, and my face. I just need to check if they look... sharp enough, I guess? \n\nAnd if I feel like they don't—if I look even a little bit soft—it just completely messes with my head. I lose my edge. I just don't feel confident or, like, command-ready for the shift, you know? \n\nI know it probably sounds really shallow to worry about my face and waist like that, especially for a guy. But you kind of have to look the part to feel the part, right? I'm really just trying to be professional and ready for anything. I hope that's okay to say. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If you were told that your appearance was already exactly how a firefighter should look, and that you were medically healthy, would you still feel the need to control your eating the way you do?\n\nPatient: Um... man, I'd really like to say that I'd just drop the routine completely. Like, if someone told me I looked exactly right and was totally healthy, I'd want to just say, 'Okay, great,' and go back to eating whatever. Because I know that's what I should do, right? I really do want to be reasonable about all this. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know for sure. I haven't really been in a position where I felt like I could just... stop worrying about it. I'm really trying to be cooperative and do this right, I hope you know that. Is it okay that I don't have a solid answer for that one?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel significant distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight, even when you know you’re eating healthy and staying fit for the job?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess I do still worry about it, even when I know I'm doing all the right things with my diet and training. I really try not to let it get to me, because I know I'm putting in the work, but... \n\nIt's like I mentioned before, about checking the mirror before a shift? I just... I just don't feel as capable or ready to take charge if I feel like I'm looking soft. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to get so hung up on something like that when I know I'm fit, but... Um... well, outside of the firehouse... I guess I do stay in a bit more than I used to. But it's not like I'm hiding or anything! I just... sometimes when my friends want to go out, it usually revolves around getting drinks or going to a restaurant.

Um, I hope I'm in the right place for this. \n\nBasically, I guess I'm here because... well, I've been trying to get on the lieutenant track at the firehouse, and I started out just trying to eat a bit cleaner to prepare for that. Which is a good thing, right? Just trying to be healthier. But, um, I guess it sort of turned into me cutting my meals way down. \n\nLike, lately I'll usually just do black coffee and maybe a protein shake during the day, and then a pretty small dinner. And I've been doing that even on shift days, when I know for a fact I've burned way more energy than that. It's probably not a huge deal, but I just want to make sure I'm not messing anything up, I guess? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this way of eating been causing you any distress or affecting your performance at the firehouse or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, my performance is still really good. I'm still doing my job just fine, I promise. I wouldn't ever let anything put the crew at risk. \n\nI guess... well, it hasn't really caused distress, exactly. But, um, I have started ducking out of station meals a bit. You know how it is, the guys will cook a big dinner or order takeout, and lately I just sort of keep to myself when they do that. It's just easier to stick to my own stuff that way, right? \n\nBut I guess it does mean I'm... well, I'm a little less part of the team than I used to be. Which I know probably doesn't sound great, and I feel a little bad about it. But work-wise, everything is totally fine. Is that... is that the kind of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about food, your weight, or your shape quite a bit, or feeling anxious if you can't stick to your planned meals?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm anxious, exactly. I mean, I definitely like to stick to my routine, but that's just because I want to stay disciplined, you know? I hope that's okay to say. \n\nI guess I do think about my weight a bit more than I used to. It's just... well, sometimes if I step on the scale and it's up even just a pound—like maybe after one of those big station meals if I do eat with the guys, or even if I just drank extra water—I get this... this sort of tight feeling in my chest. Almost like a little panic, I guess? \n\nIt's silly, I know. But I just get so worried that I've completely blown it, and that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft or that I'm out of control. Which I'm not, I promise. I just want to look the part of a leader. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding too crazy.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you plan, like during those station meals, do you ever find yourself exercising a lot more or cutting back even harder on food afterward to make up for it?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I might try to just be a little more mindful the next day, you know? Like maybe stick to just the protein shake and skip dinner if I had a big lunch with the guys. But that's just normal balancing things out, right? Like, anyone would do that if they overate. \n\nAnd as for exercising, I just do my regular workouts. I have to stay in shape for the job, so I always make sure I get my training in. But I don't go crazy or anything. I just want to make sure I'm doing the right things to stay fit for duty. That's a healthy way to handle it, isn't it? I hope I'm answering this right.\n\nDoctor: After eating more than you had planned, like during those station meals, do you ever make yourself vomit or take laxatives, diuretics, or any other medications to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. \n\nUm... I know some people do, but that just seems really... well, extreme, I guess? And I have to be ready for calls at the firehouse, so throwing up or taking pills like that would totally mess up my system. I couldn't risk doing that to my body when the crew relies on me. \n\nI just stick to, you know, being careful the next day like I mentioned. I promise I don't do any of that other stuff. That's a good thing, right? I'm really trying to be smart about how I handle all this, I swear.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a much larger amount of food in a short period than you intended, like feeling like you've lost control and eaten a lot all at once?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not. I'm actually really careful to make sure that doesn't happen. I mean, even if I have a slightly bigger meal with the crew like I was saying, I never just... lose control or anything. I always know exactly what I'm eating. \n\nI think I'd be really ashamed if I just let myself go and ate a huge amount all at once. I try to stay really disciplined, so I keep a pretty tight grip on things. That’s a good sign, right? That I'm not losing control like that? I just want to make sure I'm on the right track with all this.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for eating this way because you're trying to lose weight or change your body shape?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess I just want to make sure I don't get out of shape. I wouldn't say I'm trying to be, like, super skinny or anything, because I need to be strong for the job. But... yeah, I do worry a lot about gaining. \n\nHonestly, if I step on the scale and it's up even just a single pound—even if I know it's just from extra water or right after one of those big meals with the crew—I get this really tight panic in my chest. It just feels like I've completely blown it, you know? I get so worried that the other guys are going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that they'll think I'm just totally out of control. \n\nI just really want to look the part and prove I'm reliable. It's normal to want to stay sharp and not get soft in my line of work, right? I hope that doesn't sound too crazy. I'm just trying to be the best firefighter I can be, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your main concern is about gaining weight, or is it more about maintaining control and discipline with your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Um... I think it's a little bit of both, maybe? But mostly, it's just about the weight. \n\nI mean, the control part is important because it helps me... well, not gain weight. But the real fear is definitely seeing that number go up. I just... I get so anxious about getting heavier. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to be this obsessed with a few pounds, especially for a guy, but I just want to make sure I'm staying fit for the job. That makes sense, right? It's better to be focused on not gaining weight than to just not care at all, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to eat this way even if your weight stayed exactly the same?\n\nPatient: Um... that's a good question. I guess... I'm not totally sure? \n\nIf I knew for a fact my weight wouldn't change at all, maybe I wouldn't have to be quite so strict. But... I don't know, it's hard to imagine just letting go of all the rules. They kind of keep me grounded, I guess. \n\nBut honestly, if the weight wasn't an issue, I'd like to think I could just eat normally like the rest of the guys. I really just want to be healthy and do my job well. It's totally normal to want to stick to a routine once you're used to it, right? I'm not trying to be difficult or anything, I just... I haven't really thought about it like that before. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the same need to stick to your current eating routine if a doctor told you that your current weight and body composition are perfectly healthy and appropriate for your job?\n\nPatient: Um... wow, I mean, if a doctor told me I was perfectly healthy, I'd really want to believe them. I'd want to just be normal and let it go. But... honestly? I think I'd still struggle to drop the routine. \n\nBecause... well, it's not just about the medical numbers on a chart, I guess. It's... I feel a little embarrassed even admitting this, because I don't want to sound vain, but... it's about what I see, too. Like, when I look in the mirror right before heading to the station, I always kind of zero in on my waist, and my face. I just need to check if they look... sharp enough, I guess? \n\nAnd if I feel like they don't—if I look even a little bit soft—it just completely messes with my head. I lose my edge. I just don't feel confident or, like, command-ready for the shift, you know? \n\nI know it probably sounds really shallow to worry about my face and waist like that, especially for a guy. But you kind of have to look the part to feel the part, right? I'm really just trying to be professional and ready for anything. I hope that's okay to say. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If you were told that your appearance was already exactly how a firefighter should look, and that you were medically healthy, would you still feel the need to control your eating the way you do?\n\nPatient: Um... man, I'd really like to say that I'd just drop the routine completely. Like, if someone told me I looked exactly right and was totally healthy, I'd want to just say, 'Okay, great,' and go back to eating whatever. Because I know that's what I should do, right? I really do want to be reasonable about all this. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know for sure. I haven't really been in a position where I felt like I could just... stop worrying about it. So I guess I'm just not sure how I'd actually react in that scenario. \n\nI'd definitely try my absolute best to ease up, though, especially if you or another doctor told me I needed to. I'm really trying to be cooperative and do this right, I hope you know that. Is it okay that I don't have a solid answer for that one?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel significant distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight, even when you know you’re eating healthy and staying fit for the job?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess I do still worry about it, even when I know I'm doing all the right things with my diet and training. I really try not to let it get to me, because I know I'm putting in the work, but... \n\nIt's like I mentioned before, about checking the mirror before a shift? I just... I get really fixated on my waist and my face. If I don't look lean enough, or sharp enough, it just kind of sits in the back of my mind all day. It really throws off my confidence. I just don't feel as capable or ready to take charge if I feel like I'm looking soft. \n\nI know it probably sounds silly to get so hung up on something like that when I know I'm fit, but... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself the right way, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound too superficial. Is it normal to get a little stressed about that kind of stuff?\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend thinking about your weight or body shape on a typical day?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess I haven't really timed it or anything. I try to stay focused on my job and everything else I have going on, so it's not like I'm just sitting around obsessing over it all day. I'm usually pretty busy.\n\nBut, you know, it does pop into my head... maybe a few times a day? Mostly just around meals, or when I'm getting dressed for a shift and doing that quick mirror check I told you about. I try to just acknowledge it and move on, so it doesn't take up too much of my time. \n\nI really try to keep a good balance and not let it consume my thoughts, because I know that wouldn't be healthy. Does that sound like a normal amount to you? I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself preoccupied with your weight or body shape even when you're not thinking about food or meals?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess it does happen outside of just eating. Like I was saying about the mirror checks before work... I'll just be getting ready, not thinking about food at all, but I'll catch myself really focusing on my waist or how my face looks. \n\nIf I don't look... I don't know, sharp enough, or lean enough, it just kind of lingers. It really messes with how confident I feel when I walk into the station. I just want to feel command-ready, you know? Like I look the part of someone people can rely on in an emergency. \n\nI try not to let it ruin my whole day, but it definitely distracts me sometimes when I'm just trying to do my job. I hope that doesn't make me sound too vain or anything. It's just about feeling prepared, I think. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your weight or body shape caused any problems in your relationships, at work, or in other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Um... I wouldn't say it's caused any real problems. I'm definitely still doing my job, and I never let it get in the way of a call. I always make sure I'm 100% there for the public and my crew when it counts. \n\nBut... I guess, maybe a little bit at the station? It's just... firefighters eat together a lot, you know? It's a big part of the culture. And lately, I've just been kind of... ducking out of the station meals. When the guys order out or cook a big dinner, I usually just keep to myself and eat my own prepped food in the other room. \n\nI just... I don't want to make a big deal out of it or make them feel weird, so it's easier to just step away. But I guess it does make me feel a little less part of the team than I used to be. I miss hanging out with them in the kitchen. \n\nI'm still a good firefighter, though, I promise. I just prefer to handle my own meals right now. Is that... is that okay? I mean, a lot of guys bring their own food to work, right?\n\nDoctor: Have your concerns about your weight or body shape affected any other parts of your life, like your mood, your relationships outside of work, or your overall enjoyment of life?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, my mood is usually pretty good. I try to stay positive, you know? I don't want to be a downer for anyone else. \n\nAs for relationships outside of work... I'm single right now, so there's no one I'm really affecting there. I still see my friends sometimes, though maybe not quite as often as I used to. But that's just because of the job, right? Being a firefighter takes a lot out of you, and the schedule can be pretty demanding. It's totally normal to just want to rest on your days off instead of going out. \n\nI definitely still enjoy my life. I love my job, and I'm proud of what I do. I guess maybe I'm a little more focused on my routine right now than I used to be, but that's just because I'm trying to be disciplined. That's a good thing, isn't it? Being disciplined and taking care of yourself? I really hope I'm not giving you the wrong impression. I'm doing okay, I promise.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations, even outside of the firehouse, because of concerns about your weight or how you look?\n\nPatient: Um... well, outside of the firehouse... I guess I do stay in a bit more than I used to. But it's not like I'm hiding or anything! I just... sometimes when my friends want to go out, it usually revolves around getting drinks or going to a restaurant. \n\nAnd, you know, I just don't really want to be the guy who sits there not drinking or eating what everyone else is having. It just feels awkward. I don't want to make them feel uncomfortable because I'm trying to stick to my routine, so sometimes it's just easier to say I'm too tired from shift and stay home. \n\nIt's not really about how I look, I don't think. It's more about just... avoiding the hassle of explaining my choices, you know? I don't want to be a burden or ruin their good time. That's considerate, right? Just letting them have fun without me making things weird?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
